# Dockhand roadmap

This is the source of truth for the features each release carries, and in what order. [Design v3](design-v3.md) defines behavior, [architecture](architecture.md) maps it onto the code, and [usage](usage.md) is the guide. A feature's design is in [designs](designs/); bugs, improvements, and cleanup are [issues](https://github.com/herbygillot/dockhand/issues); what was decided, and what was declined, is in [decisions](decisions.md); what each release carries is in its notes in [releases](releases/); what was done, and why, is in Git's history, and the documents this roadmap once linked are at tag `docs-archive-2026-10-25`.

## How work gets in

The kind of work decides its way in (the person's "Go for it", 2026-11-04 12:31Z, on the blind-spots thread's proposal):

- **A bug** is a `bug` issue. It goes straight to implementation, is fixed on main with a test, and rides the next candidate.
- **An improvement**, a wording or a small change in behavior, is an `improvement` issue. It lands when convenient, with no design.
- **A feature** is designed by architecture, its design in docs/designs, then placed here in a named release as an entry of a few lines with a `feature` issue. A release's features are fixed when it opens, and its candidate is cut once they land; a feature placed later goes to the next release, unless the person names this one.
- **A core change**, rearchitecting such as a package split, a new layer, or a protocol or schema change, is a `core` issue, in a release with no large feature, accepted as everything passing as before.
- **Cleanup**, a rule moved to its owner, a duplicate removed, a file split, is a `cleanup` issue, taken in the change that next works in its area.

A release's issues carry its milestone. An open bug or improvement doesn't hold a candidate: at the cut, its milestone moves to the next release. Only harm found in a candidate's pass, or a regression since the last release, makes another candidate. A commit that fixes an issue closes it with `Fixes #N`. Architecture's reviews run when a release opens, each finding going to cleanup or to the next core release. Only a list that carries features gets a coalescing run across the roadmap; its bugs and improvements become issues as they are.

After every release, before the next release's features are fixed, prioritization goes through every open issue with no milestone and brings the person a short list, each issue with a recommendation: a milestone, or closed as not planned. The person decides; nothing is placed or closed on prioritization's word, and a decline that is a decision gets its line in decisions. That round, architecture's review, and fixing the release's features happen in one sitting. An issue that has been through two rounds without a milestone is closed as not planned, unless the person names it. `cleanup` issues are exempt: the round closes only those whose code is gone. The first round follows v0.8.0's release (the person's ask and "Agreed", 2026-11-10 17:33Z and 17:34Z).

Implementation writes its own done entries, with their commits and measures, on main, and a done entry leaves once its release's candidate is cut, the release's notes carrying it from there and the commit that removes it saying so. Prioritization places features and opens the issues for what reaches it, starting each placement from main's roadmap (the person's "Do #3, #5, #2 & #1", 2026-10-31 07:45Z).

## Where dockhand stands

The whole loop is built and used for real work, MacPorts pull requests and dockhand's own port among them. On main:

- **Branches:** `setup`, `start`, `adopt` (`--pr` for someone's pull request), `path`, `archive`, `clean`, and `undo`.
- **Authoring:** `update`, `create`, `checksums`, `revbump`, and `edit`; `ship` updates, checks, and submits, and `bump` does it asking nothing.
- **Understanding:** `status`, `outdated`, `diff`, `impact`, `explain`, `config`, and `open`.
- **Checks:** `check` in Tart (macOS 12 through 27, each with an Xcode image), on the fork's GitHub Actions, through a command of your own, or in a MacPorts prefix on this Mac (`local`); `logs`, `retry`, `shell`, `artifacts`, `wait`, and `cancel`.
- **Review and submit:** `tidy`, `rebase`, `submit`, `review`, and `decide`; `serve` runs queued checks and the daily look at the selected ports.

v0.7.0 is the latest release (2026-11-03, 47f00138), its notes in [releases/v0.7.0.md](releases/v0.7.0.md). v0.8.0 is on main, its candidate waiting for the person's word.

  Each step is measured the same way when it lands. The shapes are implementation's, with architecture.
- **One capacity contract** (Codex's review of 275125ff, finding 4's first step; one batch). Three accountings each read `e.capacity(provider)` and decide alone: `SeeAllThrough` (engine/session.go:119) reserves channel slots for its own batch, `awaitSlot` (engine/slots.go:33) counts live processes' runs before a foreground check, and serve (engine/serve.go:378) keeps `inUse` and `inFlight` for its own runs, so a batch's runs don't count serve's, nor serve's a batch's, and only `awaitSlot` sees across processes (no race reproduced; the structure makes one easy to write). One reservation, taken and released through slots.go over the store's live runs, is what every driver uses, and serve's maps go, a check that waits for it saying once what holds it wherever it waits, as rc2's line for serve's queue does (G14); the batch drive being three days old, it's cheapest now. It takes the code-organization review's serve finding 3, each worker saying a problem once and the daily look off the loop, where serve's loop is rewritten (from Taken when touched). Done on main 2026-10-31, the reservation part: a check's lease is its slots (engine/slots.go `reserve`, coord `AcquireIn`), taken only where each provider its plan builds on has fewer checks than its capacity held by live sessions, queued or running, decided and taken in one transaction; the foreground check (`awaitSlot`, which returns the lease `driveLeased` drives with), the batch, whose own channels went, and serve, whose `inUse` went, all take it, and a wait says once in the run's journal what holds it (`sayHeld`, for serve's queue and the rest alike). A batch's wait now names its holders as a foreground check's does, "providers.command.capacity is 2, and check-2 (a foreground dockhand, pid …) and check-3 (…) are using it", where it said "which the batch's other checks are using (capacity 2)"; G13's grep for "waits for a slot" holds. Its test holds one slot from another session and sees a batch's check wait, say so, and take it once let go. - **Two databases on one checkout don't take each other's checkpoints** (rc2's full stage, the Prime-time session, sent here by the coordinator on 2026-10-31 at 08:41Z; not harm, a refusal that changed nothing, and in v0.6.0 too; about a fifth of a batch, inferred; done on main 2026-10-31, 826ef485: `history.Prepare` numbering past the highest name the checkout has under `refs/dockhand/checkpoints/`, the store taking a number at or above its own next; `history.KeptRefs` the one rule clean's `dropRefs` and recovery's `dropCheckpointRefs` drop by, the index ref's parent read with `git.Parents`; and `history.TakenName` the refusal, "tidy-N is another dockhand's checkpoint on this checkout, so nothing was changed; run again", read from the ref conflict). A checkpoint's ref, `refs/dockhand/checkpoints/tidy-N` or `rebase-N`, is named by its database's counter (store/sqlite `NextCheckpointNumber`, the highest recorded plus one), and a `DOCKHAND_DB` copy carries its original's counter, so a copy run on its original's checkout hands out the same names: a G13 copy made tidy-783 to 788 on dhtest's clone, dhtest's database, at 782, gave out 783 again, and tidy failed with "reference already exists". The refs move in one transaction (engine/tidy.go, `UpdateRefs` with the branch), so nothing moved. Where the names meet, a database can delete another's: clean drops every ref its merged branch's checkpoints name, whatever it holds (engine/clean.go `dropRefs`), and recovery drops a stopped checkpoint's index ref whatever it holds (history/history.go `dropCheckpointRefs`), so one database's restore point can go with another's merge. So:
  - a checkpoint's number skips a name already under `refs/dockhand/checkpoints/` in the checkout, so the name people type stays tidy-N; refs carrying the database's identity are declined, since they rename every ref a person has and need a migration for nothing a skip doesn't give;
  - clean and recovery drop a checkpoint's refs only where they hold what it kept, its `Before`, and for the index ref a commit on `Before`, as recovery already checks for the history ref;
  - where another process took the name in between, tidy and rebase say the name is another's on this checkout and nothing changed, and to run again.
  A copy run on its original's checkout still has two databases recording one clone's branches, which no naming fixes: docs/development.md's scratch runs already give a copy its own clone and worktrees, the heavy-runs rule standing on that, and the usage that names a copy says so with its archives (Chores).

What holds it is measured first, since a live heap kept and pages Go hasn't returned to macOS look the same from outside: a `DOCKHAND_HEAPPROFILE` file, beside `DOCKHAND_CPUPROFILE` (cmd/dockhand/main.go:39–60), takes the in-use heap profile after a collection as the process exits, and in serve also when its queue empties after a look. Implementation takes it on the main Mac, with `tools/heavy`, over a look of a like number of ports, and the profile says the fix. Where the look's own state outlives it, the engine's run-wide caches among the candidates (`others`, `https`, internal/engine/engine.go:280–287, whose entries serve's days keep in their maps though `httpsKept` ages them), that state goes once the look and its checks end, scoped to the look or forgotten after it. The next rc's serve row samples an hour after its day's work, and tens of MB then is the bar, with the profile kept beside the row. Done on main 2026-11-03, in the commit after 08873662. Measured first, on the main Mac under `tools/heavy`, a scratch copy of the database and caches, `outdated --mine` over 1,116 ports, the look serve's day makes: 139 s, 458 MB at most resident, 209 MB peak footprint, and a live heap of 37 MB at its end, 30 MB of it `internal/github`'s conditional cache, which held every GitHub answer in memory beside its directory for the process's life; the engine's `others` and `https` caches didn't show. So both: the conditional cache keeps an answer on disk alone where it has a directory, read from it when asked again, and in memory only where there's none or the disk won't take it; and serve, once its queue empties after work, a look or a check, gives what Go keeps back to macOS (`debug.FreeOSMemory`) and calls `ServeOptions.Settled`. `DOCKHAND_HEAPPROFILE` (`settings.WriteHeapProfile`) writes the in-use heap after a collection at the command's end and in serve's Settled; docs/development.md's Profiling says it, with the measurement. Tests: the profile written and refused where its directory is missing; an answer on disk alone with a directory and in memory without; serve settling once, after its check ended. The serve row of the next rc is the measure of the resident size an hour after the day's work.

  - a `--note` is kept with the branch before submit's gate (note 9). Without a terminal, `submit --note '…'` showed the note in its preview, stopped at "without a terminal, --yes submits exactly what is shown" (command/submit/submit.go:108–112), and a later `submit --yes` opened the pull request without it, though the help says the note "is kept with the branch" (submit.go:51). The note reaches the branch only once the pull request is made (engine/submit.go:1241–1243). A `--note`, given or emptied, is saved with the branch before the gate, unless `--plan`, which changes nothing; a refused or cancelled submit says so: "Nothing was submitted; the note is kept with the branch, and submit --yes includes it". `--title` stays as it is, a pull request's. Where every group it combines is dockhand's edits alone (`FromEdits`), the squash adds Generated-By, as a chained group does; where any is a hand edit, none, as now. - **A tree's evidence is every build its checks made, a port's variant builds beside its default build** (field testing's note 10 from the curl fix, check-772 +gnutls +sectrust and check-773 +ssl +sectrust passing on the files check-774 then built with defaults, the description showing check-774's row alone and the variants item unticked; architecture's design, /mnt/project-files/plan/pr-evidence-variant-builds-2026-11-03.md, read against main 6c6f6069, sent here by the coordinator on 2026-11-03 at 01:41Z; confirmed here; not harm, evidence a pull request leaves out; about a tenth of a batch, inferred; any time before v0.8.0's candidate, after the variant check of an unchanged port above). Which checks count is the rule `EvidenceFor` (engine/evidence.go:102) has: every finished check of the same files, whatever it was asked to build, with `--on`, `--build`, `--only`, `--also` or `--variants`, baselines aside; not a check of an earlier tree, since an edit makes new files; each result one build in one environment, counted where that environment is still what it was, so a variant build made on tahoe alone is evidence on tahoe alone. The gap is one rule: the evidence keeps the primary check's targets and its omitted ones (`Of`, evidence/evidence.go:257; `Judge`, :346), earlier checks filling only cells those lack by target ID (`fill`, :1008), so check-772's "curl +gnutls +sectrust" and check-773's "curl +ssl +sectrust" vanished once check-774 built "curl". The ask (D17) and required environments don't change, and a variant build never widens or narrows them.
  - `Judge` takes, after the primary's targets and its omitted ones, each target an earlier check planned that the evidence lacks, newest check first, and fills its cells as it fills the others'. A port's rows go together, its default build first, then its variant builds in the order their checks ran, newest first, each cell naming the check that built it, as earlier checks' cells do (`Built`, `sharedRuns`). A variant build beside a default build some check made is an extra, as an `--also` port is: shown where it ran, and in the ask's other environments its cell is `outsideProfile`'s exclusion, "outside what the newest check of these files asked for", never unchecked, so its absence never blocks a submission. Where no check of the files built the port's default build, every one having chosen variants, the variant builds are the port's coverage, as today when such a check is the primary. The target's role carries it: a build the primary didn't plan joins as `model.Also` where the port has a default build in the evidence, and with the primary's own role where it hasn't.
  - the newest result of a build in an environment stands, every cell's rule today: a check that fails `curl +ssl +sectrust` on tahoe after one that passed it replaces the pass, and the reverse. A failed variant build that stands is a failed extra, as a failed `--also` port is (ExtraFailed, branchstate/attention.go:357): status says it, the variants item is unticked, the table shows it, and submit asks to accept it or check again. `--accept` names the build as the table does, `--accept 'curl +ssl +sectrust'`, so accepting it doesn't accept the default build; `--accept curl` keeps its meaning, the default build. `AcceptedFailure`, keyed by port name (`allBuilt`, engine/body.go:264–270), is keyed by target ID, a bare port name reading as its default build. Where the failed build is the port's only coverage, it is the changed port's own failure, as today.
  - the variants item is ticked where the evidence holds a variant build of a changed port and every variant build of changed ports that stands passed wherever it ran, `--variants each` and chosen variants alike, from any check of the files. Its note names what was built and where, "(Dockhand built curl with +gnutls +sectrust and with +ssl +sectrust added to the default variants, in check-772 and check-773)", keeping its each form, "with each of +gnutls, +ssl, +universal added to the default variants", where a check built each. A variant build that failed and stands leaves it unticked, even accepted, the note saying "(Dockhand built curl with +gnutls +sectrust, which passed, and with +ssl +sectrust, which failed in check-775)". `--tested-variants` ticks on the person's word as now, and submit's question (command/submit/submit.go:167) is skipped where dockhand's tick stands, asked where it doesn't. The preview lists the variant builds behind the tick. `VariantsBuilt` (evidence.go:1119) reads the evidence's targets, the builds with a variant spec whose port the branch changes, their outcomes and checks, rather than the primary's `EachVariant`.
  - status reads the same evidence: a tree whose checks all passed, variant builds included, is CheckPassed; a failed variant build that stands is ExtraFailed, "Next: dockhand submit --accept 'curl +ssl +sectrust'", or a check again; an earlier check's variant build stays in the detail's table after a later defaults check. Not proposed: weighing "most important" variants (the person's `--tested-variants` stays for that judgment); carrying variant builds across trees beyond the carried-over reuse every build has (model.Carried).
  - Usage docs. docs/usage/publishing.md: the paragraph beginning "A narrowed check adds evidence for the targets and environments it actually built." gains, after "Submission combines applicable results for the same files": "every build those checks made, a port's variant builds beside its default build, each a row of Tested on naming the check behind each cell; a variant build beside the default build is required nowhere and shows where it ran. The newest result of a build in an environment stands, so a check that fails a build an earlier check passed replaces the pass." The paragraph on `--tested-binaries` and `--tested-variants` becomes: "`--tested-binaries` and `--tested-variants` state that you made the corresponding manual checks; do not infer them from an install alone. A passing variant build of a changed port answers the variants item from what was built: `check --variants each`, or chosen variants, in any finished check of the same files; the item's note names each build and its check, `(Dockhand built curl with +gnutls +sectrust and with +ssl +sectrust added to the default variants, in check-772 and check-773)`. A variant build that failed and stands leaves the item unticked and the note says which failed. The generated Tested on section …", the rest as it stands. The acceptance list, after "`submit --accept <port>`: Accept an eligible recorded failure, or a new port's …", gains "`submit --accept '<port> +variant'`: Accept a variant build's failure by the name the table gives it; `--accept <port>` is the port's default build." - **The main help names dockhand's issues page, at its foot** (the person's ask, sent here by the coordinator on 2026-11-03 at 19:20Z: "Have Dockhand mention its repo issues page in its help output."; not harm, polish; about a fiftieth of a batch; any time before v0.8.0's candidate). The address is `buildinfo.ProjectURL + "/issues"` (buildinfo/version.go:12), as the commit message's and pull request's dockhand links are, not a second copy of the address.

A subcommand's help is the quick reference for its arguments and flags, read many times a day, and the same line under every command is noise; the main help is where a person goes looking for where to report something. Not `dockhand usage`'s guide either, which the README's section and the main help already point past.

- **A check's staged tree, input.tar, doesn't outlive the attempt that staged it** (Prime-time's v0.7.0 acceptance, sent here by the coordinator on 2026-11-03 at 22:13Z: when serve resumes an interrupted check, the interrupted attempt's input.tar, 173 MB, stays in ~/.dockhand/logs/check-N/ after the check ends; not harm, disk alone; about a fiftieth of a batch, inferred; any time before v0.8.0's candidate).
  - today Tart stages the revision into `<logs>/check-N/<environment>-<attempt>/input.tar` and removes it by a `defer` when `Execute` returns (tart/provider.go:624–625). So it sits beside the log for the whole build, though the guest has its own copy once `launch` has uploaded and unpacked it (provider.go:706, 1020–1031). A process stopped mid-build (serve stopping, an upgrade's serve taking over, the Mac restarting, kill -9) runs no `defer`, and the resumed check builds in the next attempt's directory (checks/runner.go:674, 872), so the stopped attempt's archive stays. Log cleanup compresses a finished check's `.log` files only (`cleanLogs`, engine/retention.go:95; buildlog/files.go:76), so an open branch's newest check in an environment keeps the archive until its directory goes. The local provider removes its own once it has unpacked it (local/local.go:358–368), its window staging alone.
  - after, three small changes. Tart removes input.tar as soon as `launch` returns, copied into the guest or not, rather than when `Execute` ends, so a stop during the build leaves none, only one during staging or the upload. - **`wait --for` and `check --for` follow a check for a bounded time, and exit 4 while it continues** (the openssl4 new-port session's note 4, macports/macports-ports#36375 on v0.7.0, sent here through field testing on 2026-11-04 at 02:53Z, from Later; the person's "As shown" on prioritization's triage card at 02:58Z; architecture's design, sent here by the coordinator on 2026-11-04 at 03:14Z, /mnt/project-files/plan/wait-for-2026-11-04.md; not harm; about a quarter of a batch, inferred; any time before v0.8.0's candidate).
  - the author's words: "wait has no time bound. Agent tool calls are capped (10 minutes for me) and macOS has no `timeout` command, so I ran `dockhand wait check-N` in the background to a log and polled for "Passed for commit" / "failed for commit". Something like `wait --for 9m` that exits with a clear code (passed / failed / still running) would suit agent use. A human at a terminal doesn't need this, so weigh how much agent use matters."
  - today exit codes are one table, `refusal.Kind.Exit` (refusal/refusal.go): a check failed 2, attention 3, interrupted 130, every other refusal 1; `term.ExitError` carries a code for an outcome that isn't a refusal (`status --attention`'s 3, `term.ExitAttention`); docs/usage/agents.md's table (line 112) lists 0, 1, 2, 3 and 130, and a check's end exits 0, 2, 3 or 130 from `checkrun.Report`. `wait [check]` (command/runcmd/queue.go) is `checkrun.Wait`: a finished run is reported; otherwise `Follow` → `Engine.SeeThrough` (engine/checkservice.go:136, over `checks.Service.SeeThrough`, checks/session.go:52), which drives the run here where nothing leads it (no serve, no holder: `Lead.Here`, `awaitSlot`, `DriveLeased`) and follows it (`waitFor`) where a serve or another process does. Ctrl-C while following is `refusal.Interrupted`, "stopped following check-42; it continues", Next `dockhand wait check-42`; while driving here it stops the check, keeping finished results. `check -d` queues and returns, serve taking it or `Settings.Drain` starting `dockhand serve --drain` in a session of its own (host/cleanup_unix.go `startDrain`, drain.log beside the database): "check-42 is queued; a background worker (PID 4242) will run it. Follow it with dockhand wait check-42." A drain takes queued runs and runs left running by a driver that's gone (checks.Service.Candidates, recovery.go:21). `status` never waits: a running check is `check_standing: checking` and one of `active_checks`. An agent's loop today is the note's: `check -d`, then `wait` in the background and a poll of its log.
  - after: `--for <duration>` on `wait` and on `check` follows the check for at most the duration, and where it hasn't ended by then, exits 4 saying what it is doing and that it continues. Nothing `--for` follows runs in the command's own process: with `--for` the check is always serve's or a background worker's to run, so returning at the bound stops nothing.
  - `wait --for 9m [check]`: a check that ended already, or ends within the bound, is reported as today, exit 0, 2, 3, or 130. Still queued or running at the bound, exit 4:
    ```
    check-42 is still running after 9m: building socat on macOS 26; it continues.
    Next: dockhand wait check-42 --for 9m
    ```
    "building socat on macOS 26" is the journal's latest progress line, what the redrawn status line shows (`journal.show`'s `EventProgress`, `EventWaiting` and the rest); a check that hasn't started reads "check-42 is still queued after 9m, waiting for a slot on tart (check-41 is using it); it continues", the journal's last wait as it was said. The Next names the same bound. Where `wait` alone would have run the check here (nothing leads: the run is queued, or left running by a process that's gone), `wait --for` starts a background worker as `check -d` does (`Settings.Drain`) and follows it: "No dockhand serve is running; a background worker (PID 4242) runs check-42. Following it for 9m." Where the worker can't start, exit 1: "check-42 is queued and nothing runs it: Dockhand could not start a background worker (<error>). Run dockhand serve, or dockhand wait check-42 without --for runs it here". Ctrl-C before the bound: as today, 130, "stopped following check-42; it continues". `--json`: the check object `wait` emits today (branch, revision, plan, `run` with its state, `targets` empty until it finishes), and the envelope's `exit_code` 4 with `error` carrying the line above; a script reads `exit_code` and `run.state`, and nothing else is added.
  - `check --for 9m` queues the check as `-d` does (serve takes it, or a background worker starts), then follows it as `wait --for` does: exit 0, 2 or 3 when it ends within the bound, 4 when it doesn't, with the same line. The lines before the follow are `-d`'s ("check-42 is queued; a background worker (PID 4242) will run it"), then "Following it for 9m." A check an existing one covers (`Followed`) is followed for the bound. A baseline `check.baseline` queues after a failure within the bound is said with its name, "check-42 · baseline queued, since check.baseline is on: dockhand wait check-43 --for 9m follows it", and left to the worker, as `-d` leaves it. Refused together: `-d` ("-d returns at once and --for follows for a while; use one") and `--plan` ("--plan builds nothing and waits for nothing"). One command for an agent whose tool calls are bounded: `dockhand check --for 9m`, then `dockhand wait --for 9m` until the exit code isn't 4.
  - the duration: Go's forms, `9m`, `90s`, `1h30m`. Refused: a bare number ("--for 9: name a duration with its unit, as 9m or 1h") and zero or less ("--for 0s: the bound is above zero"). No maximum and no config default: a human at a terminal doesn't need it (the note's own words), and an agent passes it each time.
  - the code, settled once across wait, check and status: 4 is a new row of the one table, `refusal.Kind` `StillRunning = "still-running"`, `Exit` 4, an outcome (`Outcome` is already true for any code but 1), raised by `wait` and `check` alone, with Next the wait above; `term` gains no constant (`ExitCode` reads the kind). `status` never exits 4: it doesn't wait, its JSON says `checking`, and `--attention` stays 3. At a check's end, `check` and `wait` exit 2 and 3 as today. 75, sysexits' EX_TEMPFAIL, was considered and declined, since the table is dockhand's own small numbers, read from the docs.

A bounded follow never drives: `checks.Service` gains `Observe(ctx, id)`, its `waitFor` alone, which `Wait` and `check --for` use after `LeadOf` says who leads; where nobody does, `Settings.Drain` first, then `Observe` (a worker takes a moment to lead; observing doesn't care who does). `check --for`: `RunQueued` with the enqueue path, then `Observe` with the bound; the two refusals before anything is captured.

Neither runs the check in its own process: with no serve, a background worker runs it."; docs/usage/checks.md, "Run in the foreground or queue it" (line 285), after the `-d` paragraph: "`--for 9m` on `check` or `wait` follows for at most nine minutes and then exits 4 if the check is still queued or running, saying what it is doing; the check continues, with serve or a background worker, never in the command's own process, so `wait --for` starts a worker where `wait` alone would have run the check here. `dockhand wait check-42 --for 9m` follows it further."; docs/usage/unattended.md, "Queue a check, or run a continuing worker" (line 101): the example becomes `dockhand check --branch jq-update --head --for 20m` and `dockhand wait check-42 --for 20m`, with "`--for` bounds the follow: exit 4 says the check is still running and continues; repeat `wait --for` until the exit code is 0, 2, or 3.", and the sentence at line 82 gains "A check still running at `--for`'s bound exits 4."; docs/usage/recovery.md, the `wait check-42` paragraph (line 118): "With `--for`, wait never drives the check here: where nothing else runs it, it starts a background worker, as `check -d` does, and follows."; help: `wait`'s and `check`'s Long gain the sentence, and the flag reads `--for duration   follow for at most this long, then exit 4 if the check is still running; it continues`.

- **`rebase --plan` says what a rebase would replay, drop, or stop at, and moves nothing** (part 5's shared-flag contract, 2026-10-22, from Later; the person's "could make it into 0.8.0", 2026-11-04 00:19Z; architecture's design, sent here by the coordinator on 2026-11-04 at 02:31Z, /mnt/project-files/plan/rebase-plan-2026-11-04.md; not harm; about a fifth of a batch, inferred; any time before v0.8.0's candidate).
  - today `dockhand rebase` (`engine.Rebase`, verbs.go:163) refuses uncommitted edits, fetches master, finds the merge base, and replays the branch's commits one by one with `git merge-tree --write-tree` (`git.Replay`), each written as an object before anything moves: a conflict names its paths and leaves the branch as it was, a merge commit is refused, a commit whose change master has is dropped; then the branch, its checkout, and a checkpoint move, "Rebased jq-1.8.1 (3 commits) from master 3f2a1c9 onto 9b8c7d6", `--json` giving branch, from, to, up_to_date, commits (a count), and checkpoint. Every other `--plan` shows what a command would do and changes nothing.
  - after: `rebase --plan` does the first half and prints it. It fetches master, as rebase and `outdated` do, which moves no branch; replays into objects nothing references, which `git gc` prunes; moves nothing and writes no checkpoint. Each commit's fate, in order:
    ```
    Rebase jq-1.8.1 (3 commits) from master 3f2a1c9 onto 9b8c7d6 (fetched just now):
      replay   a1b2c3d jq: update to 1.8.1
      drop     b2c3d4e jq: fix checksums (master 9b8c7d6 has this change)
      replay   c3d4e5f jq: whitespace
    Nothing moved. dockhand rebase replays them and keeps a checkpoint.
    ```
    A conflict stops the replay where rebase would, saying where and what's left untried: "  conflict c3d4e5f jq: whitespace, in sysutils/jq/Portfile" then "dockhand rebase would stop here, with jq-1.8.1 as it is; resolve it by hand with git rebase 9b8c7d6, or ask for help on the PR."
  - what rebase refuses, the plan says in its place, changing nothing: a branch already on master ("jq-1.8.1 already starts from master 9b8c7d6"), uncommitted edits ("jq-1.8.1 has uncommitted edits to sysutils/jq/Portfile; rebase would refuse until they're committed (dockhand tidy)"), a merge commit ("b2c3d4e is a merge commit, which dockhand doesn't replay"), and a branch master has every change of ("nothing to rebase; dockhand clean jq-1.8.1 removes it once its pull request is merged"). The notes rebase prints after moving are said as what would follow: the pull request's old commits submit would replace, and the older builds the commits' Generated-By keep.
  - `--json` keeps today's keys and adds `"plan": true` and `"replay"`, a list of `{"id", "subject", "outcome"}`, outcome `replay`, `drop`, `conflict` (with `"paths"`), or `untried`; `"commits"` stays the count replay would make.
  - implementation notes, architecture's: `git.Replay` returns what it did beside the head, `[]Replayed{Commit, Outcome, Paths}`, stopping at a conflict as now, so rebase's words and the plan's read one record; `engine.rebase` splits into `planRebase` (refusals, fetch, merge base, replay) and the move, `Rebase` calling both and `PlanRebase` the first, `Rebased` gaining `Replay []Replayed`; command/tidy/rebase.go's `--plan`, "show what the rebase would replay, and onto what, and change nothing".
  - Usage docs, same commit: docs/usage/branches.md (line 177, the rebase example and the paragraph after it) adds `dockhand rebase --plan` before the rebase, with "`--plan` fetches master and says what the rebase would replay, drop as already on master, or stop at, and moves nothing."; docs/usage/recovery.md (line 286, where a rebase changes the base) names `rebase --plan` as the way to see a conflict before it happens; docs/usage/agents.md's `--json` catalog, rebase's keys, gains `plan` and `replay`; `rebase --help`, the flag.

  - Done on main 2026-11-04, in the commit after c484be4e: `git.Replay` returns `[]Replayed{Commit, Outcome, Paths}` beside the head, outcomes `replay`, `drop`, `conflict`, and `untried`, and refuses a merge commit before replaying anything, as `git.MergeCommitError`; `engine.planRebase` holds the refusals, fetch, merge base, and replay, each refusal carrying the plan's words (`Rebased.Refused`) and rebase's error, and `rebase` moves what it planned; `PlanRebase` returns the plan, writing nothing (an up-to-date branch's base isn't recorded). `rebase --plan` prints each commit's fate and "Nothing moved. dockhand rebase replays them and keeps a checkpoint.", a conflict with what it leaves untried and "dockhand rebase would stop here…", a refusal in its place, and the notes rebase would print after; `--json` adds `plan`, `replay`, and `refused`. engine aliases `Replayed` and the outcomes, since commands don't import git. branches.md, recovery.md, and agents.md (rebase's keys, which it hadn't listed) say it, and the contract test's `noPlan` entry for rebase goes. - **`tidy --restyle` writes path-style dependencies, in a commit of its own** (field testing's note 15, 2026-10-31, from Later; the person's "I think yes on this one.", 2026-11-04 02:10Z; architecture's design, sent here by the coordinator on 2026-11-04 at 02:31Z, /mnt/project-files/plan/restyle-path-style-2026-11-04.md; not harm; about a third of a batch, inferred; any time before v0.8.0's candidate).

Separately the `path-style` notice (`commitrules.CheckDependencies`) offers, for a `port:` dependency a change adds, the form the tree writes most (`engine.treeSpec`, `path:bin/pkg-config:pkgconfig`) where another port can satisfy it (`engine.providers`: the `-devel` sibling or `otherProviders`, each whose `conflicts` names the port), reading only the lines the change touched and rewriting nothing.
  - after: `--restyle` makes, after the whitespace commit, a commit "<port>: path-style dependencies" that rewrites each literal `port:X` word of a `depends_*` option, at the top or in a variant body, where the `path-style` rule would offer a form: `providers(X)` names another port and `treeSpec` gives the spec. The rewrite is the spec, in place, nothing else on the line moved; a `path:`, `bin:`, or `lib:` entry, and one written through a variable, are left. Where whitespace finds nothing and this finds something, only this commit is made, and the reverse. Its body names each rewrite, "pkgconfig as path:bin/pkg-config:pkgconfig, which pkgconf satisfies too", and its note reads "rewritten by --restyle, each port depending on the same ports".
  - its own proof, since a dependency's value changes: the Portfile is evaluated before and after as the whitespace commit's is, and compared with dependencies read by phase and port alone, `path:bin/pkg-config:pkgconfig` as `port:pkgconfig`; every subport must evaluate the same but for that, and each rewritten entry must resolve to the port it named. A Portfile that evaluates otherwise, or can't be evaluated, is left and said under "Not restyled:", "restyled path-style, jq evaluates otherwise".
  - the notice agrees with the rewrite: the same `providers` and `treeSpec` decide both, so a rewritten line is no longer a `port:` entry and the notice doesn't fire on it; one it couldn't rewrite keeps the notice.

Keep that choice out of a change when you only want its functional edits."; docs/usage/hand-edits.md, where restyle or the `path-style` notice is named, one sentence that `--restyle` writes the form the notice offers; `dockhand explain path-style` (commitrules/explain.go:65) adds "`tidy --restyle` rewrites every such dependency the Portfile writes, in a commit of its own."; `tidy --help`'s `--restyle`: "restyle each changed Portfile whole and write its path-style dependencies, each in a last commit of its own".

- **A declared library nothing links is said, and an undeclared link names the pkg-config that brought it in** (the abook session's follow-up, 2026-10-28, and gnome-voice's note 9, 2026-10-24, from Later and Taken when touched; the person's "could make it into 0.8.0", 2026-11-04 00:19Z; architecture's design, sent here by the coordinator on 2026-11-04 at 02:31Z, /mnt/project-files/plan/declared-library-nothing-links-2026-11-04.md; not harm; about half a batch, inferred; any time before v0.8.0's candidate).
  - today the guest's link scan (guest.tcl's `links`) lists a passed target's installed files with `port contents`, reads each Mach-O's libraries with `otool -L`, and asks `port provides` whose each is, recording `model.Link{Binary, Library, Port, Declared}`; the host (`engine.linkFindings`) takes the target's `depends_lib` as `Declared`, and `evidence.UndeclaredLinks` says each port a build links and doesn't declare (`link-undeclared`, a hold), or "links openssl3 and zlib's libraries, each declared in depends_lib". `guestprogram.Protocol` is 1. Nothing reads what a declared port installs, so abook's `port:libiconv`, which nothing it built links, went unsaid.
  - the guest reads what each declared port installs: for each `depends_lib` entry as the guest's MacPorts resolves it (as `path_dependencies` reads them, `port:` and `path:` alike, by the port each names), `port -q contents <port>` once, keeping its dynamic libraries (Mach-O files under `lib/` ending `.dylib` or `.so`) and its pkg-config modules (each `.pc` file's module name, the `-l` names of its `Libs:` line, and the modules of its `Requires:` line); and it counts the Mach-O files the target installed. The result gains `declared`, a list of `{port, libraries, modules}`, and `machos`, the count; the protocol becomes 2, and a result without them, a reused older check's, is unread, said as links are today. A `port contents` that fails leaves that port out, silently.
  - the rule, `unlinked-dependency`: a passed build's declaration of port D is unlinked where all four hold: (1) the Portfile's own text declares D in a `depends_lib` option, as `portfile.portDependencies` reads it, a `port:` entry or a `path:` entry the guest resolved to D, so a dependency a PortGroup adds (python313 under python, perl5 under perl5, ruby under ruby) is never said; (2) D installs at least one dynamic library, so a port of programs alone, git or cmake, is never said; (3) the target installed at least one Mach-O, so a pure Python, Perl, or Tcl module or a script port is never said; (4) no Mach-O the target installed links a library D installs, in any environment the check built, so one linked on one release and not another is linked. A notice, never a hold: a library loaded with dlopen, or a program the port runs, declared in depends_lib by a maintainer who chose to, is theirs to keep. Its words: "declares libiconv in depends_lib, which nothing it built links: a header-only library or a build tool belongs in depends_build, and a program it runs or a library it loads at run time in depends_run". It appears beside the link line in the check's report, status's detail, and submit's preview, under the target; `check --json` carries it in the links section as `"unlinked": [{"port": "libiconv", "option": "depends_lib", "line": 23}]`; `dockhand explain unlinked-dependency` gives the rule. tao, declaring tcl and zlib and linking neither, is said, rightly.
  - the companion: for each undeclared link of port P (the hold as today), where a declared port D's modules name P's library, directly in a `Libs:` line or through `Requires:` followed module to module among the declared ports' modules, the words add how and the two ways out: "gnome-voice links cairo's library, which it doesn't declare, through gtk4's pkg-config Libs (gtk4 requires cairo); depends_lib-append port:cairo declares it, or -Wl,-dead_strip_dylibs drops what it doesn't call". The hold stands; only its words change. `--json`'s link finding gains `"through": {"port": "gtk4", "modules": ["gtk4", "cairo"]}`. A module whose `Requires:` names one no declared port installs stops there.
  - cost: `port contents` is a registry read, milliseconds a port; forty `depends_lib` entries add under a second to a build of minutes; nothing is fetched.

A clone is kept only where the guest finished and `Plan.Keep` was asked (provider.go's `keepClone`); every other is deleted as its attempt ends, and `clean` or the branch's next check removes a kept one. A running check's clone, `dockhand-check-<run>-<release>-<attempt>` (`clonePrefix`, `vmName`), is reachable only through the progress line's "building in dockhand-check-… (TART_HOME=… tart exec … …)". `tart exec` is used once more, in `provision.ReadyAgent`, setup's readiness probe.
  - a shell in a running check's guest: `dockhand shell check-12` while check-12 runs on Tart opens a shell, or runs the command, in the attempt's clone: the engine names the clone from the run's live execution and the release (`--on` chooses where several build), the provider reaches it as a check does (`Reach`, `AwaitSSH`), the shell starting in the port's work directory. Nothing is stopped afterwards; the check owns the VM. `--test` is refused there: "--test reruns the test phase in a kept clone; check-12 is running".
  - its results stay evidence only where the shell couldn't have touched them: the run records each entry, `Entered []Entry{At, Release, Command}`, and a target's result on that release counts as entered where its build hadn't finished at the entry, by the guest's step stamps: a target installed before the shell opened stands; the one building, and every one after it, is recorded as it is and marked. No question is asked; off a terminal, the command after `--` as today.

- **Cleaning a branch removes the clones its checks kept** (field testing's note 13, the Trac 74620 session, on v0.7.0-rc7, 2026-11-04, sent here by the coordinator at 11:11Z; not reproduced by the session, confirmed by prioritization's reading of main; harm in kind, about 32 GB a clone left where the check's own words say clean removes it, and after `clean --delete` beyond any removal of dockhand's; in v0.7.0 for `--keep` clones, and v0.8.0's keep_failed default, 5a2d3f1e, makes every failed Tart check keep one; about a tenth of a batch, inferred; before v0.8.0's candidate).
  - what happened: `clean trac-74620-sectrust --yes` (merged) and `clean trac-74620-master --delete --yes` (archived) removed the worktrees, the branches and the fork branch, and left both branches' kept clones, dockhand-check-run-exnx4ydrs4q243xp-tahoe-1 (check-771) and the one with feggospylmt4ugmk (check-774), about 32 GB each; neither plan named them, though the check's plan says "clean or the branch's next check removes it" (view/writers.go:45) and a failed check "Its clone is kept for …; clean, or the branch's next check, removes it." (command/checkrun/run.go:473). The session removed them with `tart delete` under TART_HOME=~/.dockhand/tart.
  - why, read on main: a clean that names branches plans their worktree, Git branch, fork branch and check branches (`planCleanBranch`, engine/clean.go:202) and no leftovers, by design, "Branches named are cleaned alone: what other checks left is for a clean that names none" (command/clean/clean.go:133), and the Long says "nothing a check left"; only a clean naming none plans `PlanLeftovers`, every kept clone of every branch. `clean --delete` (`PlanForget`/`Forget`, engine/forget.go:38, :87) removes the fork, worktree and Git branch, then forgets the record, and touches no provider; with the record gone, `leftoverRun` finds no check of this checkout for the clone, so clean and automatic cleanup both keep it as possibly another database's ("no check of this checkout made it", clean.go:868), for good. The branch's next check removes its kept clones (`forgetKept`, clean.go:793, from checks/queue.go:134), which a merged or deleted branch never has; automatic cleanup removes a kept clone after a day, or a failed one after seven (`KeptFor`, `KeptFailedFor`, cleanup.go:77, :81), while the record stands.
  - after: a clean naming a branch plans that branch's checks' kept clones, and any environment its checks left when their process died, beside its other steps, in its own block's words, "  remove   Tart clone dockhand-check-run-exnx4ydrs4q243xp-tahoe-1, kept by check-771 for dockhand shell" (`forgetKept`'s selection, `KeptAt` set, or a leftover, by `Run.Branch`), each removed under its check's lease as `RemoveLeftovers` does, one whose check is running kept and said; other branches' leftovers stay a clean naming none's. `clean --delete` plans the same, lists them in its plan, and removes them before forgetting the record; one it can't remove (its check is running, or the removal fails) keeps the record, and says so, as a dirty worktree does, so no clone is left that no check of this checkout claims. `--plan` lists them; `--json` puts them under each branch's `leftovers`, as the unnamed clean's top-level `leftovers` reads; the one-line summary counts them.

  - why, on main: Generated-By goes only on a commit whose files are exactly dockhand's edits (`TidyGroup.FromEdits`, engine/tidy.go:63), and `commitmsg.Compose` (macports/commitmsg/compose.go:45) leaves out the combined commits' earlier Generated-By otherwise. The curl fix's note 11 (done on main 2026-11-03) added it back to a `--message` squash only where every group is dockhand's edits alone (tidy.go:598); a squash with no `--message` takes its one group's message (tidy.go:601), and a group holding dockhand's update and a hand line isn't `FromEdits`, so main drops it too. The preview says the trailers it keeps and adds (note 12), not one it drops.
  - after: a squash keeps Generated-By where any commit it combines carried it, or any group it combines is dockhand's edits, with or without `--message`, since the combined commit still holds what dockhand wrote; one made only of hand edits carries none, as now. This changes note 11's rule ("where any is a hand edit, none"): the trailer says the commit holds dockhand's work, which a hand line beside it doesn't undo, and leaving it out hid that from reviewers. A plain tidy of a hand edit, not squashed, still writes it without the trailer. tidy's preview says it as "keeps: Generated-By: Dockhand v0.7.0 (…)" as it says the others.
  - v0.8.0's "Dockhand declares what a check found linked" writes atuin's line itself, as dockhand's edit, so this case's hand edit goes; the rule above covers the hand edits that remain.

  - Around it: a moved Git port handed down the archive path (`planObservedArchives`) with its download in the store, fetched once; `tarballMoved`'s asset read folded in, one request; `fidelity.ScopedVersion` expecting the move's options (master_sites, distfiles, distname, worksrcdir, extract.suffix, fetch.type, checksums, the use_ option) and still refusing anything else; `Result.TarballMoved` becoming `Result.SourceMoved` (from, to, asset, the submodule paths it carries, the lines written), `Update.Fixed`'s line and `--plan` saying it; where nothing's chosen, the update goes through as today, and the `github-tarball` rule becomes a `source` notice, "release 2.5.0 carries mindforger-2.5.0.tar.gz, which lacks deps/cmark-gfm, so the port stays on its clone", never a hold or refusal. The comparison pairs a moved Git port's before, the forge's archive of the old commit (`SourceArchiver`), with the asset, submodule paths said once as added with the move, and a tarball port's old tag's tarball with the asset where names differ. `checksums` alone writes and fills a main port's missing block, saying so.
  - `update --source choose|keep|releases|archive`, `choose` by default, and `[update] source` beside `keep_http`: `keep` moves nothing; `releases` or `archive` asks for that target and refuses, naming the test that failed, where the asset doesn't pass.
  - Not taken: `archive` ports to `releases` by default (a `make dist` tarball differs from the repository, 2,376 ports, a judgment each); a source judged by its name; a vendored submodule checked against the commit pinned (the check's build is the test); subport-scoped fetches, modes set in an `if`, pinned commits, and forks, which stay, said.
  - Tests: portfile goldens (mindforger 2.0.0 to 2.5.0 less the comment; a `.tbz` asset writing `use_bzip2` and `extract.suffix`; a default-named asset writing one line); the candidacy rules over asset and member lists (binary bundles, two compressions, ambiguity); portedit with a fake forge and fixture tarballs, tarball to releases, tarball to archive, Git to releases, Git staying for a missing submodule, a custom hook, a pinned commit, `--source keep`; `checksums` writing a block; the engine's words and commit body. Acceptance: the tarball-to-archive row gains the releases case, on a fixture repository with a release asset under the test account, as D-N2's is, an `ACCEPT_` variable naming it, and a Tart check of the moved port.

### v0.9.0, the core release

v0.9.0 restructures the code first, then takes the core changes from Codex's review of dockhand's workflows (2026-11-04), once architecture has assessed it, with no large feature; it's accepted as everything passing as before (the person's "Go for it", 2026-11-04 12:31Z). Nothing here goes in before v0.8.0's candidate is cut. Its issues are the [v0.9.0 milestone](https://github.com/herbygillot/dockhand/issues?q=is%3Aissue+milestone%3Av0.9.0)'s.

- **The source chooser split by owner, with forge's asset descriptor, and `HostMacPorts` read once** ([#16](https://github.com/herbygillot/dockhand/issues/16); Codex's review of 03b380f1, finding 2; about three twentieths, inferred; nothing a person sees). The archive's inventory goes to `project`, the requirements and the selection to a small `macports/sourcechoice`, and portedit keeps the edit; forge's `Assets` gives a descriptor with each asset's size.
- **Codex's workflow review's core changes,** placed here as architecture's assessment names them.
- **A modelled context takes no host answer: what the table lacks is absent, marked `closed`, on a Mac and off one** ([#110](https://github.com/herbygillot/dockhand/issues/110); design: [designs/closed-model.md](designs/closed-model.md); architecture's model-from-snapshots assessment, item F, with the Linux design's closed model, at the person's "I'm fine with A-F", 2026-11-10 13:17Z; a core item, about a tenth of a batch plus the gating survey, inferred). A host read in any modelled context is answered as an empty Mac answers it and marked `closed`, inconclusive unless `Tolerate` explains it; the Mac's native context is untouched; oracle.md's decision 1 is settled. After #101 and #108, gated on one before-and-after survey counting the update and checksums contexts it newly turns inconclusive (lightened 2026-11-10 at the person's adoption of architecture's right-sizing of the modelled context, #109 no longer a prerequisite). Answered as an empty Mac, the person's answer to the Linux design's first question; the rest of that design is cut.
- **Standard output wraps only dockhand's own prose: `Out` raw, `Prose` wrapped** ([#94](https://github.com/herbygillot/dockhand/issues/94); design: [designs/prose-stream.md](designs/prose-stream.md); architecture's review of dcef3282, approved by the person 2026-11-06 03:16Z; a core item, about a fifth of a batch, inferred). `term.Streams`' `Out` stays raw and a new `Prose` wraps and colours; dockhand's sentences, tables and views write to `Prose`, anything from a file, a tool or a template to `Out`, and `Unwrapped()` goes with no bridge, so forgetting costs a long line rather than a broken Portfile. About 250 sites renamed after an audit, one mechanical commit; `TestOutIsNeverWrapped` and a `SameAtAnyWidth` helper over six commands. v0.8.0's four unwrapped writers are #93.
- **`dockhand send-facts`: a Mac's toolchain, hardware and macOS facts for a bug report, shown, then sent as a secret gist** ([#83](https://github.com/herbygillot/dockhand/issues/83); design: [designs/send-facts.md](designs/send-facts.md); the person's ask in the architecture thread, 2026-11-05 02:19Z, placed here at their "Plan it for 0.9.0", 02:46Z; about a quarter of a batch, inferred; a small feature beside the restructure, not a large one). The probe tools/facts runs, under this Mac's port-tclsh, with `sw_vers`, `uname` and a few sysctl facts, scrubbed and shown before a gist is made; the login gains the `gist` scope. Narrowed 2026-11-10 with the right-sizing: the gist no longer becomes a facts-table row, so there is no `tools/facts sent`.
- **`dockhand backup <file>`: a consistent copy of the database** ([#92](https://github.com/herbygillot/dockhand/issues/92); design: [designs/db-backup.md](designs/db-backup.md); the person's ask in the project chat, 2026-11-05 21:12Z, placed here at their "Place it for 0.9.0", 22:00Z; about a tenth of a batch, inferred; a small feature beside the restructure). A verb in the Occasional group that copies the database with `VACUUM INTO`, never opening the engine, so it backs up a database before its first migration; it never overwrites, names a directory's copy by date, and says what the copy lacks. Restore stays by hand, documented. v0.9.0's upgrade note opens with it. Automatic copies, or copies at events such as a schema update, are weighed apart and not placed.

### v0.10.0

- **Canary checks: `check --also a,b --set name=value`** ([#50](https://github.com/herbygillot/dockhand/issues/50); design: [designs/canary-checks.md](designs/canary-checks.md); the openssl4 new-port session's note 5, moved from v0.9.0 when it became the core release; about a batch, inferred). Each `--also` port is built with a Portfile or PortGroup option set, as `port install libssh2 openssl.branch=4` sets it, in the check's builds alone: a library branch checked against the ports that use it, with no edit to their Portfiles. Such a build is its own target, an extra, never evidence for the branch's changed ports.

The person's "You can create the test repository" (2026-11-01 13:28Z): on main, in the commit after edda4b92, host/make-source-fixture.sh (edda4b92) makes it under herbyg-test unless named, idempotent, and G36, full stage, reads it: by default the update to 1.1.0 leaves the clone and says the release's .tgz carries deps/sub, `--source releases` moves it there, and a Tart check of the moved port passes; it waits on the script's run on the M1 as the test account and ACCEPT_SOURCE_FIXTURE.

## Later

Unplaced features, improvements, and core changes are the open [issues with no milestone](https://github.com/herbygillot/dockhand/issues?q=is%3Aissue+is%3Aopen+no%3Amilestone). Cleanup taken when its area is next touched is the [`cleanup` issues](https://github.com/herbygillot/dockhand/issues?q=is%3Aissue+is%3Aopen+label%3Acleanup); a change in an area reads them first. The oracle's remaining phases ([#20](https://github.com/herbygillot/dockhand/issues/20)) and the host-independence foundations ([#21](https://github.com/herbygillot/dockhand/issues/21)) run on the Mac as it allows, independent of a release's order.

## Maintenance

- **Release candidates.** An rc is tagged when the M1 is free to start its acceptance, after a short window that gathers what's found since the last, so fixes found hours apart make one tag, not two (the person's "Do #3, #5, #2 & #1", 2026-10-31, item 2: rc3 followed rc2 by an hour, and part of rc2's stages were done again). Once main takes the next release's work, the release's later candidates are tagged from `release/v0.N`, made at the last candidate, each fix picked onto it as it lands on main, so the last candidate's acceptance still stands for what didn't change. Only harm found in a candidate's pass, or a regression since the last release, makes another candidate (proposed 2026-10-31, adopted with the work process, 2026-11-04).
- **Acceptance rows move with the behavior they test.** A commit that changes what a person sees changes its row in the same commit, and before it lands runs those rows on the main Mac, in the heavy-run turn, with `tools/heavy tools/acceptance/quick.sh --rows "<affected>"` (the same list, item 1: rc2's quick stage lost hours to B1, B2, and A10 expecting rc1's behavior, fixed in 013d5f70). CI's acceptance self-test proves the runner and the harm sweep against a stand-in, not the rows against the build, and a lint of the rows wouldn't have caught those three; a run of the no-VM rows against main is v0.8.0's chore.
- **The main Mac's heavy runs take turns** (the same list, item 5): implementation's measures and scratch builds, the survey's runs, and field testing's checks share the Mac and its database, so they keep one rule for whose turn it is, each on its own `DOCKHAND_DB` copy with its archives beside it. The turn is `tools/heavy <command>`, a lock on `~/.dockhand-heavy.lock` that says who holds it to a run that waits; `make gate` and `make test-corpus` take it themselves (docs/development.md, "Heavy work on a shared Mac").
- **MacPorts compatibility.** Evaluator tests pass on Base 2.12.6, and CI runs them against the package of whichever release MacPorts currently points its users at, and a weekly job runs them against base's master (231f48c1). Version adapters follow the Base design prospective (2026-09-24): the current release plus a pinned master preview. Keep Base and PortGroup compatibility distinct ([evidence](macports-compatibility.md)).
- **Images.** Twelve images are made and checked: base and Xcode for macOS 12, 13, 14, 15, 26, and 27. Re-probe the facts table when an image is rebuilt; the 2026-09-27 probes found every earlier row unchanged. Golden Gate needs Tart 2.39.0 or newer.
- **Package boundaries.** Extend import checks when touching a boundary that matters; the command and engine boundary tests are the models. Don't split packages by size alone. CI runs `make deadcode`, which fails on a function nothing reaches, tests included. Keep test-only exports documented as such. After a milestone, a sweep runs the tool without `-test`: what it lists, only tests reach, and each is test support, documented as such, or dead. The tool can't see an unused exported method on a type that reaches an interface or reflection, so a sweep checks those by references too.
- **Relocations.** A batch that moves a rule to its owner lists, in its note, the call sites it switched to the owner's rule, and closes with a grep across internal/ for the old rule's shape, its predicate, sentence, or switch, deleting what it finds or saying why it stays. Each of the 5897a82 review's high findings would have been caught by that grep on the day (its section 5). A rule is moved only once every consumer carries the facts it needs and stops judging beside it: findings 3, 4, 6, and 9 of Codex's review of 8e314b96 are a consumer judging beside its owner, so a move adds an agreement test across the consumers, check, review, status, submit, ship, and serve, where they apply. The engine was 24,704 lines in 68 files at 8e314b96; it's measured again at v0.7.0's rc1, and not split for size alone.
- **Dependencies.** Minor releases go on main when they build and the gate passes, as 3706d086's did; a major version is measured first, by building main against it, and is an issue like any fix (the person's "Let's go with you recommendation.", 2026-10-24 18:31Z). A new Go library is taken where it makes sense, to gain major functionality, improve performance, or eliminate logic dockhand keeps repeating (the person's "Change the dependencies rule: I'm open to new Golang libraries and dependencies where it makes sense…", 2026-10-31 23:48Z, in prioritization's thread, replacing the library survey's preference for none), weighed in the item that brings it: what it replaces, the modules it brings with it, each a go.vendors entry in dockhand's port, and its maintenance.
- **GitHub's budget.** Requests to its API are paced at 750 a minute, under its documented 900. A whole `outdated --mine` spends about 1,800 of the 5,000 an hour the person's account has, a budget shared with the GitHub CLI.
- **Test throughput.** The cost unit is the MacPorts interpreter. Keep per-test timing visible, and prefer simulated clocks for time-driven tests; v2's `workflow` tests show what wall-clock budgets do under load.
